#!/bin/zsh

# Run step-measure-tolerance on all STL/STEP pairs under tests/ and print a table
# with per-group (suffix) summary rows.
# Distances displayed in mm. OCCT converts STEP units to mm internally.
set -euo pipefail

SCRIPT_DIR="$(cd "$(dirname "$0")" && pwd)"
PROJECT_DIR="$(cd "$SCRIPT_DIR/.." && pwd)"
BINARY="$PROJECT_DIR/target/release/step-measure-tolerance"

# Build release binary
echo "Building step-measure-tolerance (release)..." >&2
cargo build --release --bin step-measure-tolerance --manifest-path "$PROJECT_DIR/Cargo.toml" 2>&1 | tail -1 >&2

ROW_FMT="%-40s %12s %12s %12s %12s %10s %10s %10s %8s %8s %10s %10s %10s %10s\n"

print_header() {
    printf "$ROW_FMT" "Test Case" "Vtx Max mm" "Vtx Avg mm" "Ctr Max mm" "Ctr Avg mm" "Max Dim" "Ang Max °" "Ang Avg °" "Nodes" "Faces" "STEP Area" "STL Area" "STEP Vol" "STL Vol"
    print_sep
}

print_sep() {
    printf "$ROW_FMT" \
        "$(printf '%0.s-' {1..40})" \
        "$(printf '%0.s-' {1..12})" \
        "$(printf '%0.s-' {1..12})" \
        "$(printf '%0.s-' {1..12})" \
        "$(printf '%0.s-' {1..12})" \
        "$(printf '%0.s-' {1..10})" \
```

The script discovers pairs by looking for `*.stl` and checking if `*.step` exists with the same basename. So after renaming, STL files that previously matched `foo_medium.stl` → `foo_medium.step` won't find the step file since it'll be `foo.step`. The STL files need to keep their quality suffixes, but the script needs a different pairing strategy. Actually, looking more carefully, the user said to remove quality from `.step` file names. The script auto-discovers pairs - so the STL files like `plate_with_hole_100x50_low.stl` won't find a matching `.step` automatically.

//! Integration tests for Stage 1: mesh input and validation.
//!
//! Tests that all STL/STEP test file pairs pass stage1 with consistency checks
//! and --compare validation, and that intentionally bad files fail correctly.

use brepper::config;
use brepper::stage1;

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Helpers
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

fn manifest_dir() -> String {
    env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR").to_string()
}

fn config_for_stl(stl_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--stage=1", "-q"]).unwrap()
}

fn config_for_compare(stl_path: &str, step_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    let mut config =
        config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--compare", step_path, "--stage=1", "-q"])
            .unwrap();
    config.load_compare_step().unwrap();
    config
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Macros for generating test cases
```

// Macros for generating test cases
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Generate a test that runs stage1 (read + validate) on a given STL file.
macro_rules! test_stl_stage1 {
    ($name:ident, $rel_path:literal) => {
        #[test]
        fn $name() {
            let stl = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $rel_path);
            let config = config_for_stl(&stl);
            stage1::stage1(&config).expect("stage1 should pass");
        }
    };
}

/// Generate a test that runs stage1 with --compare against a STEP file.
macro_rules! test_stl_step_compare {
    ($name:ident, $stl_path:literal, $step_path:literal) => {
        #[test]
        fn $name() {
            let stl = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $stl_path);
            let step = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $step_path);
            let config = config_for_compare(&stl, &step);
            stage1::stage1(&config).expect("stage1 with --compare should pass");
        }
    };
}

// ===========================================================================
// Good cases: all test STL files should pass stage1 validation
// ===========================================================================

Now let me also update the step_distance_table.sh script to handle the new naming where STL and STEP basenames don't match:

Read [step_distance_table.sh](../scripts/step_distance_table.sh#55-55), lines 55 to 115

> 💭 The script uses `step_file="${stl_file%.stl}.step"` which means it derives the .step file name from the .stl file name. With the new naming scheme, this won't work for files like `plate_with_hole_100x50_high.stl` → it'll look for `plate_with_hole_100x50_high.step` which no longer exists.
> 
> I need to update the script to try more flexible matching. The approach should be: if the exact same-basename .step doesn't exist, try stripping the quality suffix.
> 
> Let me update the script to handle this.

Replacing 3 lines with 7 lines in [step_distance_table.sh](../scripts/step_distance_table.sh)

The key observations are clear. Let me now look at the expected surface count. The STEP has 26 faces. A rounded cube with r=2 fillets should have:
- 6 flat faces (the cube faces)
- 12 cylindrical fillet faces (one per edge) 
- 8 spherical corner faces (one per vertex)
= 26 total faces ✓

> 💭 Let me summarize the results so far:
> 
> **Expected surfaces for rounded cube 10mm with r=2 fillets:**
> - 6 flat faces
> - 12 cylindrical fillet faces (one per edge)
> - 8 spherical corner faces
> = 26 total
> 
> **What brepper found:**
> 
> | | Coarse (820 tri) | Medium (3548 tri) | Fine (21466 tri) |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Stage 2.2 cylinders deduced | 11 | 33→11 | 91→4 |
> | Stage 2.3 spheres deduced | 8 | 8 | 8 |
> 
> 
> | Stage 2.6 selected surfaces | 88 (69 planar, 11 cyl, 8 sph) | 205 (186 planar, 11 cyl, 8 sph) | 378 (366 planar, 4 cyl, 8 sph) |
> | Stage 3 result | Fail: edge 0.39mm from STEP edge | Fail: vertex 0.21mm from STEP vertex | Fail: vertex 0.087mm from STEP vertex |
> 
> Looking at the results, the coarse mesh is missing one cylinder (11 instead of 12), while the medium mesh correctly identifies all 11 cylinders but the fine mesh drops down to just 4. All three meshes fail the final validation, though the fine mesh gets closest with errors under 0.1mm.
> 
> The core issue is that as mesh resolution increases, the cylinder detection degrades significantly—the fine mesh deduces 91 cylinder fragments but only 4 survive the selection stage, while spheres are consuming faces that should belong to cylinders. This leaves hundreds of planar faces that are actually cylindrical surfaces but aren't being properly classified.
> 
> The problem stems from three interconnected issues: cylinders fragment into many small pieces during deduction, spheres expand into cylindrical fillet regions (especially where they meet other shapes), and fine meshes generate multi-face planar groups that prevent cylinder seeding. I'm noticing the fine mesh has 71 multi-face planar hypotheses that may be blocking cylinder detection, whereas the coarse mesh has fewer of these blocking groups.
> 
> The coplanarity issue is the key—finer tessellation makes adjacent triangles on cylinder fillets closer to coplanar, causing them to merge into multi-face planar groups that can't seed cylinders. The stage 3.1 errors are a consequence of having too many surfaces (88 instead of 26), which breaks the correspondence with the STEP model's topology.

This confirms the problem clearly. The cylinders have **wildly different axes** — they're not aligned to X, Y, or Z. On a rounded cube, all 12 fillet cylinders should have axes along X, Y, or Z (4 per axis). Instead, we see axes like (0.9497, 0.0, 0.3131) which are oblique — these are **bogus cylinders fitting the spherical corner regions**.

Let me verify what the `selected` cylinders are in the fine case:

**Terminal:**
```sh
cd /Users/sethml/src/brepper && ./target/release/brepper tests/onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2_fine.stl --compare tests/onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2.step --stage=2 -v 2>&1 | grep "Cylinder " | grep "36 faces"
```

So only **4 out of 12** correct cylinders are found with 36 faces (the ones aligned to axis X and Z). The Y-axis cylinders are completely absent as clean 36-face hypotheses. Instead, those cylindrical fillet faces are being absorbed by the sphere hypotheses and by bogus tilted cylinder fits.

Let me also check how many spheres cover faces in the fine case and what fraction the spheres eat from the cylindrical regions:

**Terminal:**
```sh
cd /Users/sethml/src/brepper && ./target/release/brepper tests/onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2_fine.stl --compare tests/onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2.step --stage=2 -v 2>&1 | grep "Sphere "
```

There it is. Each sphere in the fine mesh has **1279-1384 faces** — that's about 10337 total. The model has 10847 faces total, so spheres claim 95% of all faces! The actual sphere corners should only be a small fraction. The spheres are growing way beyond their corners, eating nearly all the cylindrical fillet faces too, because every cylinder face of radius 2 also lies on a sphere of radius 2 centered at the nearest corner.

## Findings

You're right — the rounded cube is **not** a torus problem. The expected surfaces are **6 planes + 12 cylinders + 8 spheres** (= 26 faces in the STEP file). Here's what brepper actually produces:

| | Coarse (820 tri) | Medium (3548 tri) | Fine (21466 tri) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cylinders deduced (stage 2.2) | 11 (all axis-aligned) | 38 (11 aligned, 27 tilted) | 91 (4 aligned, 84+ tilted) |
| Spheres deduced (stage 2.3) | 8 | 8 | 8 |
| **Faces per sphere** | **40-52** | **~216** | **1279-1384** |
| Selected surfaces (stage 2.6) | 88 (69 planar, 11 cyl, 8 sph) | 205 (186 planar, 11 cyl, 8 sph) | 378 (366 planar, 4 cyl, 8 sph) |
| Stage 3.1 error | Edge 0.39mm from STEP | Vertex 0.21mm from STEP | Vertex 0.087mm from STEP |

### Root causes:

1. **Sphere overgrowth into cylinder regions** — The biggest problem. On a rounded cube, every cylindrical fillet has the same radius (r=2) as the corner spheres. The cylinder fillet faces genuinely lie on a sphere of radius 2 centered at the nearest corner. BFS sphere growth eats nearly ALL fillet faces — in the fine mesh, each sphere claims ~1300 faces (95% of all faces!), when the actual spherical cap at each corner is much smaller.

2. **Bogus tilted cylinders on sphere faces** — With finer meshes, faces on spherical corners produce many spurious cylinder hypotheses with tilted axes (e.g., axis=(0.95, 0, 0.31)). These pass the angular coverage check because sphere faces do span circumferentially. In the fine mesh, 84+ of 91 cylinders are bogus.

3. **Cylinder fragmentation** — Even the legitimate cylinders fragment into multiple smaller hypotheses because sphere overgrowth claims most of their faces first, leaving gaps that prevent BFS from growing complete cylinders.

4. **Coplanarity in fine mesh** — The fine mesh has 71 multi-face planar hypotheses (faces on the fillet that are so finely tessellated they appear coplanar). These block cylinder seeding.

The fundamental issue is that stage 2.6's greedy area-based selection picks the 8 massive sphere hypotheses first (they have the most area), leaving only scraps for cylinders. The sphere BFS in stage 2.3 needs to be constrained to not grow ## Findings

You're right — the rounded cube is **not** a torus problem. The expected surfaces are **6 planes + 12 cylinders + 8 spheres** (= 26 faces in the STEP file). Here's what brepper actually produces:

| | Coarse (820 tri) | Medium (3548 tri) | Fine (21466 tri) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cylinders deduced (stage 2.2) | 11 (all axis-aligned) | 38 (11 aligned, 27 tilted) | 91 (4 aligned, 84+ tilted) |
| Spheres deduced (stage 2.3) | 8 | 8 | 8 |
| **Faces per sphere** | **40-52** | **~216** | **1279-1384** |
| Selected surfaces (stage 2.6) | 88 (69 planar, 11 cyl, 8 sph) | 205 (186 planar, 11 cyl, 8 sph) | 378 (366 planar, 4 cyl, 8 sph) |

### Root causes:

1. **Sphere overgrowth into cylinder regions** — The biggest problem. On a rounded cube, every cylindrical fillet has the same radius (r=2) as the corner spheres. The cylinder fillet faces genuinely lie on a sphere of radius 2 centered at the nearest corner. BFS sphere growth eats nearly ALL fillet faces — in the fine mesh, each sphere claims ~1300 faces (95% of all faces!), when the actual spherical cap at each corner is much smaller.

2. **Bogus tilted cylinders on sphere faces** — With finer meshes, faces on spherical corners produce many spurious cylinder hypotheses with tilted axes (e.g., axis=(0.95, 0, 0.31)). These pass the angular coverage check because sphere faces do span circumferentially. In the fine mesh, 84+ of 91 cylinders are bogus.

3. **Cylinder fragmentation** — Even the legitimate cylinders fragment into multiple smaller hypotheses because sphere overgrowth claims most of their faces first, leaving gaps that prevent BFS from growing complete cylinders.

4. **Coplanarity in fine mesh** — The fine mesh has 71 multi-face planar hypotheses (faces on the fillet that are so finely tessellated they appear coplanar). These block cylinder seeding.

The fundamental issue is that stage 2.6's greedy area-based selection picks the 8 massive sphere hypotheses first (they have the most area), leaving only scraps for cylinders. The sphere BFS in stage 2.3 needs to be constrained to not grow along surfaces that are better described as cylinders — but the current algorithm has no way to distinguish "I'm still on the sphere" from "I've wandered onto a cylinder of the same radius".

Now I have the full picture. Here's the explanation:

---

Your intuition is right *in general*, but the rounded cube is a special case where it breaks down. The key geometric fact:

**On a rounded cube with fillet radius r, the cylindrical fillet edges and the spherical corners have the same radius r, and the sphere center at each corner is equidistant (distance = r) from every vertex on the adjacent cylinder fillets.**

Consider one corner sphere centered at point C with radius 2. The three cylinder fillets meeting at that corner each have radius 2, and each cylinder's axis is offset exactly 2mm from the corner. A cylinder fillet face's vertices lie on a cylinder of radius 2. But those same vertices also lie on a sphere of radius 2 centered at C — because the cylindrical surface and spherical surface are *tangent* along their shared boundary, and near the boundary the cylinder surface curves toward the sphere center at exactly the right rate.

More precisely: a cylinder fillet on a rounded cube is a 90° arc of a circle with radius r. The sphere at the corner is also radius r. The fillet meets the sphere tangentially. The cylinder axis is at distance r from two adjacent flat faces, and the sphere center is at distance r from three adjacent flat faces. At the junction, both surfaces pass through the same vertices with the same curvature.

So as BFS grows from a sphere seed at a corner:
1. **Vertex distance check**: cylinder fillet vertices are within  of the sphere because they genuinely lie on (or very close to) a sphere of the same radius centered at the corner. The further you go from the corner along the fillet, the further vertices deviate — but on a coarse mesh, the next row of fillet vertices may still be within tolerance.
2. **Centroid check**: face centroids on the cylinder near the corner are also close to the sphere surface, because the sagitta between cylinder and sphere surfaces is small near the tangency line.
3. **Re-fit**: when the sphere re-fits to include cylinder fillet vertices, the fit *improves* or stays similar because those vertices approximately satisfy the sphere equation. The least-squares sphere fit accommodates them by shifting the center slightly, and the new fit still passes all vertices within tolerance.

The re-fit validation **would** catch this if the cylinder fillet curved away from the sphere quickly — but it doesn't, because both surfaces have the same radius and are tangent. The deviation grows as ~ $d^2/(2r)$ where $d$ is the distance along the fillet from the sphere-cylinder boundary, which is tiny for nearby faces.

On a **finer** mesh, more fillet faces are closer to the boundary (smaller face size), so more of them pass the tolerance check. That's why the fine mesh has spheres consuming ~1300 faces each — nearly all the fillet faces are close enough to a corner sphere to pass.

The solid-angle coverage validation at the end is supposed to catch this (detecting that the hypothesis spans a strip rather than a patch), but the fillet faces wrap around 3 cylinder surfaces emanating from each corner, giving the direction vectors enough 3D spread to pass the eigenvalue ratio test.

Now I can see the problems clearly:

1. **Angular coverage failures**: Face 0 finds a cylinder with r=139.5mm (bogus — should be r=2!) with only 3 faces. The angular coverage correctly rejects it, but the problem is the cylinder fit itself is producing a huge radius.

2. **Trial failures**: Faces 1-5 have seed partners but trials return None — likely because the fitted cylinder from a seed pair of two nearly-parallel quads has a huge radius (nearly-flat faces → huge cylinder radius) and vertices don't pass tolerance checks.

3. **No seed partners**: Faces 144-147 are the 4 flat side faces of the cube — correct, they shouldn't seed.

The root cause: on a fine mesh, adjacent fillet quads subtend only ~2.4° of the cylinder's 90° arc. Two nearly-parallel normals with a 2.4° angle produce a cylinder axis (from cross product) but the radius estimate from just those 2 faces is wildly inaccurate — fitting a circle to vertices spanning only ~2.4° of arc gives a near-degenerate fit with huge radius. The coarse mesh works because each quad subtends ~10°, giving a much better-conditioned circle fit from the seed pair.

This is a fundamental issue with the pair-based seeding approach: **when adjacent faces subtend a tiny arc of the cylinder, the seed cylinder fit is unreliable**. The fix should be to seed from faces that are further apart on the cylinder — perhaps using multi-hop seeding (find a face 2-3 hops away that still has normals differing enough for a good fit, even though its immediate neighbors are too close in normal-space).

Here's the diagnosis:

**On the fine mesh, cylinder seeding completely fails** because the pair-based seed approach requires two adjacent faces with enough angular diversity to produce a good circle fit. On a fine mesh, adjacent fillet quads subtend only ~2.4° of the 90° arc, so:

1. The cross product of their normals IS large enough (sin(2.4°) = 0.042 > 0.01 threshold), so seed pairs ARE found
2. But the circle fit from vertices spanning only ~2.4° of arc is extremely ill-conditioned — it produces r≈139mm instead of r=2mm
3. The BFS then grows 3 faces with this bogus r=139mm fit, and angular coverage correctly rejects it (3 faces all clustered in ~7° of the bogus cylinder)
4. Every face gets marked  and we end up with 0 cylinders

The coarse mesh works because each quad subtends ~10°, giving a well-conditioned circle fit that immediately produces r≈2.0.

**The fix**: for cylinder seeding, we should look beyond immediate neighbors — collect vertices from faces 2–3 hops away so the seed circle fit has enough angular span to be reliable. Or alternatively, accumulate more faces into the trial BFS before doing the initial fit, using only the axis direction from the cross product (which IS reliable even from nearly-parallel normals) and deferring the radius fit until enough faces have been collected.

Want me to implement a fix, or would you like to discuss the approach first?

You're right — the axis direction IS correct ([0,-1,0]) and the radii are in the right ballpark (1.78-2.21 instead of the true 2.0), but the vertex deviations from the fitted cylinder are in the **1-20 millimeters** range — far exceeding vertex_tolerance of 1e-5mm.

The problem is that **the 2D circle fit from 6 vertices spanning only ~4.4° of arc is ill-conditioned**. The axis direction from the normal cross product is fine, but once we project those 6 nearly-collinear vertices onto the 2D plane and fit a circle, small numerical errors in vertex positions map to large errors in the fitted center/radius.

Coarse mesh: cross_mag ≈ 0.195 (≈ 11.25° between adjacent quad normals), and the good seeds produce r=2.0000 perfectly. The bad seeds (face pairs at fillet boundaries, adjacent to flat faces) have cross_mag ≈ 0.098 (≈ 5.6°) and produce r=32.6 — also ill-conditioned but correctly rejected.

Fine mesh: cross_mag ≈ 0.044 (≈ 2.5°) and ALL seeds produce bad radii (1.78–2.21).

So you're partially right — the cross_mag is sufficient to get the axis direction, but the circle fit is numerically unstable with only ~2.5° of arc. Let me check: on a circle of radius 2mm, vertices spanning a 2.5° arc have a sagitta (deviation from the chord) of about $r(1 - \cos(\theta/2)) \approx 2 \times (1 - \cos(1.25°)) \approx 2 \times 2.4 \times 10^{-4} = 4.8 \times 10^{-4}$ mm = 0.48 micrometers. With vertex precision of ~1e-5mm = 0.01μm, that's ~48× the noise level — should be enough for a reasonable fit! 

Let me look at the actual  function more carefully — maybe it's doing something wrong:

Searched for regex `fn fit_cylinder\b` (`**/src/stage2.rs`), 1 result
- [stage2.rs:671](../src/stage2.rs#L671)

Read [stage2.rs](../src/stage2.rs#671-671), lines 671 to 850

> 💭 I see the issue now. The algebraic circle fit is numerically unstable when the vertices span a small arc. The problem isn't the precision of vertex coordinates — it's the conditioning of the 3×3 normal equation matrix. When all points are clustered in a small arc of the circle, the matrix becomes nearly singular, leading to large errors in the solution.
> 
> Let me think about this more carefully. The 2D coordinates are not centered — they include the absolute position in the 2D projection, which means the values are large (on the order of the absolute coordinates) while the differences are tiny (on the order of the sagitta ≈ 0.48μm). This creates catastrophic cancellation in the normal equations.
>  
> Actually, I think the real issue is simpler than I thought. The 2D coordinates are absolute positions in the XZ plane (on the order of 10-20mm), and when I use Cramer's rule with these large values, the numerical conditioning falls apart. The solution is straightforward: center the data before fitting by subtracting the mean coordinates, solve for the circle, then shift the center back. This should dramatically improve the stability of the calculation. then add the centroid back after fitting to get the final circle center in the original coordinate space.

I see the problem. The algebraic circle fit uses **raw projected coordinates** without centering. When 6 vertices on a cylinder of radius 2mm are at absolute positions like (15, 12.5) in the 2D projection, the sums of squares (sx2 ≈ 1350) dwarf the signal (radius ≈ 2). Cramer's rule on this nearly-singular system gives garbage.

The fix is to **center the 2D data** before fitting. Let me remove the debug code and fix the circle fit:

```sh
git commit -F - <<'EOF'
Fix cylinder detection failure on fine meshes by centering 2D circle fit

Root cause: the algebraic circle fit in fit_cylinder() used raw projected
2D coordinates without centering. When vertices at absolute positions like
(15mm, 12.5mm) span only a ~2.5° arc of a r=2mm cylinder, the normal
equations matrix becomes catastrophically ill-conditioned — sums of squares
(sx2 ≈ 1350) dwarf the signal (radius ≈ 2), causing Cramer's rule to
produce wildly wrong centers/radii (e.g., r=139.5 instead of r=2.0).

Fix: center the 2D data by subtracting mean x,y before fitting, then add
the mean back to the recovered center. This is a standard numerical
technique for least-squares fitting.

Also adds test files:
- cylinder_10_r2_fine.stl/step: standalone r=2 cylinder with fine mesh
- part_rounded_cube_10_r2_{coarse,fine}.stl/.step: cube with 4 edges
  filleted (Y-axis aligned), useful for isolating cylinder detection

Verified:
- cylinder_10_r2_fine: 1 cylinder, 144 faces, r=2.0000, err_max=2.25e-6
- part_rounded_cube_10_r2_fine: 4 cylinders × 36 faces, r=2.0000
- part_rounded_cube_10_r2_coarse: 4 cylinders, 29 faces total
- All 313 tests pass, zero clippy warnings

---

  numerical accuracy to get a correct radius."

Based on my analysis, here's the current state of tangency detection in the project:

## 1. **Tangency Detection Algorithm (Stage 3.2)**

**Location:** [src/stage3.rs](src/stage3.rs#L925-L998)

**Algorithm:**
- **Function:** `detect_tangency()` 
- **Method:** Samples points along each edge's mesh boundary vertices and compares surface normals of the two adjacent surfaces
- **Threshold:** 2° angle threshold (cosine ≈ 0.9994)
- **Sampling:** 
  - Every 5th boundary vertex, minimum 3 samples
  - Always includes first and last vertex
  - Adaptive sampling if edge has fewer vertices
- **Result:** If **all** sampled normals agree within 2°, edge is marked `tangent: true`

Key code: Computes dot product of normals at sample points; if any point falls below the 2° threshold, the edge is not tangent.

## 2. **Test Models with Tangent Edges**

**Models that should have tangent edges:**
- [`tests/onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2*`](tests/onshape) (coarse, medium, fine) — cylinder-plane tangencies
- [`tests/onshape/part_rounded_cube_10_r2*`](tests/onshape) — similar tangencies
- [`tests/onshape/dome_hemisphere_20*`](tests/onshape) — sphere-plane tangencies

**Models with NO tangent edges:**
- `tests/onshape/chamfered_cube_10_c1_medium.*` — all planar surfaces at angles
- `tests/ccad/generated/cube.stl` — all planar
- `tests/ccad/generated/simple_cylinder.stl` — plane-cylinder meet at 90°
- `tests/ccad/generated/hemisphere.stl` — sphere-plane meet at 90°
- `tests/ccad/generated/block_with_hole.stl` — plane-cylinder edges not tangent

## 3. **Existing Tangency Tests**

**Location:** [tests/stage3_integration.rs](tests/stage3_integration.rs#L414-L465)

Current tests only verify that models **do NOT** have tangent edges:
- `cube_no_tangent_edges()`
- `chamfered_cube_no_tangent_edges()`
- `cylinder_no_tangent_edges()`
- `block_with_hole_no_tangent_edges()`
- `hemisphere_no_tangent_edges()`

**⚠️ Missing:** No tests that verify tangent edges ARE correctly detected in rounded_cube or hemisphere models.

## 4. **Chamfered Cube Model**

The chamfered cube (`chamfered_cube_10_c1_medium.stl`) has:
- **All planar surfaces** meeting at sharp angles
- **No tangent edges** by design
- It's a cube with beveled/chamfered corners (flat chamfers, not rounded)

## 5. **Current Stage 3.3 Issue**

**Location:** [src/stage3.rs](src/stage3.rs#L1272-L1275)

Tangent edges are currently **skipped** in stage 3.3 (curve computation):
```rust

- **Tangency detection works** via normal comparison at 2° threshold
- **Rounded cube models have tangent edges** but they're not being tested
- **Stage 3.3 edge curve computation fails** on tangent edges (not yet implemented)
- **Need:** Tests that verify tangent edges are correctly detected in rounded models, and stage 3.3 implementation to handle tangent cases

# Read binary STL
with open('tests/onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2_coarse.stl', 'rb') as f:
    header = f.read(80)
    ntri = struct.unpack('<I', f.read(4))[0]
    
    # Collect vertices and normals
    normals = []
    verts = []
    for i in range(ntri):
        nx,ny,nz = struct.unpack('<3f', f.read(12))
        v1 = struct.unpack('<3f', f.read(12))
        v2 = struct.unpack('<3f', f.read(12))
        v3 = struct.unpack('<3f', f.read(12))
        attr = struct.unpack('<H', f.read(2))
        normals.append((nx,ny,nz))
        verts.append((v1,v2,v3))

# Find flat faces (normals aligned with axes)
flat_faces = {}
for i,(nx,ny,nz) in enumerate(normals):
    for axis,val in [('X+',nx),('X-',-nx),('Y+',ny),('Y-',-ny),('Z+',nz),('Z-',-nz)]:
        if val > 0.999:
            flat_faces.setdefault(axis, []).append(i)

for axis in sorted(flat_faces.keys()):
    faces = flat_faces[axis]
    print(f'{axis}: {len(faces)} triangles')
    # Check vertex coordinates along the normal axis
    axis_idx = {'X':0,'Y':1,'Z':2}[axis[0]]
    vals = set()
    for fi in faces:
        for v in verts[fi]:
            vals.add(round(v[axis_idx], 6))
    vals_sorted = sorted(vals)
    print(f'  {axis[0]} values: {vals_sorted[:5]}...{vals_sorted[-5:] if len(vals_sorted)>5 else \"\"}')
    # Check max deviation from the mode
    from collections import Counter
    c = Counter(round(v[axis_idx],4) for fi in faces for v in verts[fi])
    most_common_val = c.most_common(1)[0][0]
    max_dev = max(abs(round(v[axis_idx],4) - most_common_val) for fi in faces for v in verts[fi])
    print(f'  Dominant {axis[0]}={most_common_val}, max deviation={max_dev}')
"
```

> 💭 Interesting! Each flat face has only 2 triangles (= 1 quad after fusion). That's the coarse tessellation from Onshape — each flat face has just a single quad. That means there are only 6 quads for flat faces, and 498-6=492 faces on curved surfaces. The 0 multi-face planar is explained: each flat face has exactly 1 face after fusion, so there's nothing to group.
> 
> But wait, the X+ and X- and Z+ and Z- faces have max deviation ~1e-5 from coplanar, which is right at the vertex_tolerance boundary. 
> 
> The raw STL only has float32 precision, so that deviation is just floating-point rounding error. The 76 single-face planar hypotheses in stage 2.6 break down as: 6 flat faces (each correctly identified as single-face planar) plus 70 faces on curved surfaces that weren't claimed by cylinders or spheres. Working through the math: 498 total faces minus 344 claimed by spheres and 78 claimed by cylinders leaves exactly 76 remaining, which all become single-face planar.
> 
> The real issue is that spheres are overcounting—they're growing along cylinder fillets and eating into regions that should belong to cylinders. Meanwhile, the cylinder detection is only finding 10 of the expected 12 cylinders. I thought the centering bug fix would resolve this for the coarse mesh, since the cross product magnitude should be larger (~11° between normals), but something's still off. Let me trace through which 10 cylinders were actually detected in the verbose output.
> 
> Looking at the results, I see cylinders with varying quality: the first one has a clean fit with 12 faces and radius 2.0, but then cylinders 1 and 2 are weak fits with only 5-8 faces and noticeable errors. Cylinder 3 is particularly bad with a terrible fit error. The better ones are cylinders 0, 4, and 5, which all have clean geometry and the expected radius of 2.0. The pattern suggests some cylinders are being fragmented or misidentified while others are solid.
> 
> Now looking at the axis alignment, I'm noticing a problem with the expected distribution. The X-axis cylinders (axis [1,0,0]) appear four times as expected, but the Y-axis cylinders are severely underrepresented—I'm only seeing one when there should be four. This suggests the algorithm is either missing three Y-axis cylinders entirely or misclassifying them with different axes.
> 
> The same issue appears with Z-axis cylinders. I have one properly aligned cylinder but need four total, leaving three unaccounted for. The cylinders that do show up with Y and Z axes have weak fits with higher errors, which points to a systematic detection problem rather than random noise.
> 
> This asymmetry is puzzling because a rounded cube with uniform edge fillets should treat all three axes symmetrically. The fact that X-axis cylinders fit well while Y and Z struggle suggests the sphere fitting is interfering—likely the spheres are growing too large and consuming faces that should belong to the Y and Z axis cylinders.
> 
> Wait, but cylinders are detected in stage 2.2 before spheres grow in stage 2.3, so that's backwards. The real question is why the cylinder detection itself is failing for Y and Z axes when the mesh should provide adequate angular coverage with eight quads per fillet. I'm wondering if the corner regions are creating ambiguity—where cylinder fillets meet sphere patches, the face normals might be consistent with either geometry, and detection order could be determining which primitive claims them.
> 
> Looking at the sphere face distribution, spheres 0 and 1 each have 52 faces when they should only have around 12-13 based on the expected surface area. The spheres are massively oversized, which suggests they're expanding along the cylinder fillets since both surfaces share the same radius and are tangent to each other.
> 
> Let me trace through the geometry more carefully. A vertex on the cylinder fillet far from the tangency point would be well outside the sphere's radius, so the BFS growth shouldn't reach it. But at the actual tangency line where the cylinder and sphere surfaces meet, vertices lie exactly on both surfaces, which means the growth algorithm will naturally propagate along the shared boundary. vertex_tolerance, since both surfaces share the tangent line and nearby geometry.
> 
> Wait no - the cylinder fillet surface and the sphere corner surface are DIFFERENT surfaces that HAPPEN to have complementary geometry. A point on the cylinder fillet that's far from the sphere corner would NOT be on the sphere. But near the junction, points on both surfaces converge.
> 
> How far does this "shared" region extend? Let me think geometrically. Consider the X-axis cylinder fillet at (facing +X) at, say, the edge from corner (10,0,0) to corner (10,10,0). The cylinder has axis along Y, center at (8, y, 2) (for a 10mm cube with r=2 fillets). The sphere corner at (10,0,0) has center at (8,2,2), radius 2.
> 
> A point on the cylinder at angle θ from the Z-axis can be parameterized, and I can check when it also lies on the sphere. Working through the algebra, the cylinder and sphere only intersect along a single circular arc at y=2, not across any extended area. This means vertex-to-sphere distance checks should reject cylinder faces away from that junction line, but since BFS growth propagates face by face, faces near the junction might still get incorrectly included.
> 
> On a coarse mesh, a quad spanning multiple angles might have one vertex exactly at y=2 (on the sphere) and another at y=1.something (off the sphere), which the tolerance check should catch. But if the mesh is coarse enough with only 1-2 quads in the Y direction across the cylinder range, the boundary quads at y=2 and y=8 would sit exactly at the junction points, making it hard to distinguish which surface they belong to.
> 
> The real issue is that on a coarse mesh, there might be no interior cylinder quads clearly separated from the sphere—all the cylinder quads touch the sphere boundary. With only ~8 faces around the circumference and the cylinder being short (y=2 to y=8), I'm likely seeing just 1-2 rows of quads, and both rows are adjacent to sphere regions.
> 
> Let me check how many mesh faces each successful cylinder hypothesis actually picked up to verify this pattern.
> 
> I think I see what's happening now. Both models are hitting the same wall: tangent edge curve computation isn't implemented yet, which is blocking stage 3.3 for both the fine and coarse meshes.
> 
> The full rounded cube also has that surface fragmentation problem from sphere overgrowth, but that might improve once I test it on a finer mesh. Let me quickly check how the medium mesh performs.

> 💭 OK, here's the summary:

> **rounded_cube coarse (498 faces):** 94 surfaces (76 planar, 10 cylinders, 8 spheres) — expected 26 (6+12+8)
> **rounded_cube medium (2062 faces):** 106 surfaces (75 planar, 23 cylinders, 8 spheres) — expected 26
> 
> On the medium mesh, 47 cylinder hypotheses were deduced but many are spurious (axes like [0.7071, -0.7071, 0] — diagonal directions that don't correspond to real cylinders). 
> 
> After filtering, I'm left with 23 cylinders, which is still roughly double what I should have. Looking at the axes, I can see the X-axis cylinders are clustering nicely around 4 instances, but the Y-axis is fragmenting across many candidates — some with substantial face counts like cylinder 12 with 22 faces, but others that are likely just boundary artifacts from where cylinders meet the rounded corners.
> 
> The real problem is becoming clear: the sphere's surface is being misidentified as cylinders. When you have a fine mesh on a sphere, any local patch of faces with slightly non-parallel normals can look like it fits a cylinder with the right radius, since a sphere's curvature locally mimics cylindrical geometry. The diagonal-axis "cylinders" are almost certainly these false positives from the sphere-cylinder boundaries, and the detection algorithm is picking them up because the geometric fit is actually quite good locally.
> 
> On the coarse mesh, the situation is even worse because the cylinder fillets are so short—only about 6mm on a 10mm cube with 2mm radius fillets—that there are barely any mesh faces per cylinder, making fragmentation and spurious detections more likely.
> 
> Now I'm stepping back to summarize the core findings: the tangent edge curves aren't implemented yet, which is blocking both models at stage 3.3. Beyond that, there's sphere overgrowth eating into cylinder regions, spurious cylinder hypotheses appearing on sphere faces, and fragmentation of actual cylinders into multiple pieces. The tangent edge issue is the immediate blocker and next priority, while the surface detection problems are secondary concerns that may need further refinement.

}' | sort | head -50

Here's what's happening on the medium mesh:

**47 cylinder hypotheses** found — expected 12. Breakdown:
- **X-axis: 8** (4 good with 15-27 faces r=2.0000, 4 spurious with 5 faces r≈1.99-2.00)
- **Y-axis: 11** (fragmented — 4 real fillets split into 11 hypotheses)
- **Z-axis: 14** (fragmented — 4 real fillets split into 14 hypotheses)
- **Diagonal: 14** (all spurious — 5-face r≈2.00 hypotheses seeded from sphere-corner faces)

Two distinct problems:

1. **Spurious diagonal cylinders on sphere faces**: Adjacent faces on a sphere locally fit a cylinder of the same radius. These 5-face hypotheses with axes like `[0.7071, -0.7071, 0]` are great-circle-tangent cylinders on the sphere corners. They pass angular coverage because sphere faces span a wide arc.

2. **Y/Z cylinder fragmentation**: The 4 real Y-axis cylinders are split into 6-11 hypotheses. The fragments have degraded fits (err_max ~1e-3, r≈1.99) because sphere BFS in stage 2.3 eats faces at cylinder-sphere junctions, leaving only interior cylinder faces. These interior faces then seed fragmented cylinders with different starting points.

Both problems trace to the sphere-cylinder boundary at fillet corners — the fundamental geometry challenge of the rounded cube.

> 💭 Now I understand the problem clearly. The part_rounded_cube has 4 cylinder fillets on Y-axis edges. Each cylinder fillet is tangent to two adjacent planar faces. The tangent edges (16-23) are the places where the cylinder surface smoothly meets the plane.
> 
> For a cylinder tangent to a plane, the intersection is a LINE parallel to the cylinder axis at the tangent point. This line lies at the point where the cylinder surface just touches the plane.
> 
> For each tangent cylinder-plane edge, I need to:
> 1. Identify which surface is the cylinder and which is the plane
> 2. Compute the tangent line: it's a line along the cylinder axis direction, passing through the point where the cylinder touches the plane
> 
> 
> 3. Find where the cylinder rim comes closest to the plane by projecting the plane's normal direction onto the cylinder's geometry, then trace the tangent line parallel to the axis at that closest point.
> 4. The sign of the radial offset depends on which side of the plane the cylinder sits—I need to check whether the plane normal points toward or away from the cylinder's axis.
> 
> Actually, I'm overcomplicating this. For a cylinder tangent to a plane, the outward normal at the tangent point must align with the plane's normal, so I just need to find which radial direction matches the plane's orientation. ent surfaces, the normals match at the shared boundary. So the direction from the cylinder axis to the tangent point is along the component of the plane normal perpendicular to the cylinder axis.
> 
> Let me formalize this. The cylinder has an axis direction `a`, origin `o`, and radius `r`. The plane has a normal `n` and distance `d`. The radial direction at the tangent point is the perpendicular component of the plane normal relative to the cylinder axis: `n_radial = n - (n·a)*a`, normalized to `n_radial_unit`.
> 
> For a convex cylinder tangent to a plane, the outward normal at the tangent point aligns with `n_radial_unit`, so the tangent point sits at `axis_point + r * n_radial_unit`. The tangent line runs through this point along the cylinder axis direction `a`. Let me verify this against the part_rounded_cube geometry by checking edge 16 between the cylindrical and planar faces, where the tangent line should connect vertices 12 and 13.
> 
> The vertices are at [41.1219, -10.0000, 10.5447] and [41.1219, 0.0000, 10.5447], forming a line parallel to the Y axis. Given the cylinder axis is [0, -1, 0] and the cube geometry is offset to around (38, -5, 15.5), this alignment makes sense. For tangent cylinder-plane edges, the intersection curve is simply a straight line along the cylinder axis, so I can construct a `Geom_Line` directly from the edge's vertex endpoints rather than relying on potentially degenerate surface-surface intersection results.
> 
> Now I'm reconsidering whether to attempt IntSS first for tangent cases, but the documentation warns that it can fail or produce degenerate results for tangent or near-tangent surfaces. For this specific model with cylinder-plane tangencies, I can construct the intersection analytically—it's always a line. Other tangent configurations like cylinder-cylinder would also yield lines, while sphere-plane or sphere-cylinder tangencies would produce circles. Since this model only involves cylinder-plane tangencies, the analytical approach is straightforward and reliable.
>

For line-like tangencies (plane-cylinder, parallel planes, parallel cylinders), I'll build a line from the vertex endpoints. /// Project a 3D point onto a curve and return the curve parameter.
fn project_point_on_curve(
    point: &[f64; 3],
    curve: &geom::HandleGeomCurve,
) -> Result<f64, String> {
    let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(point[0], point[1], point[2]);
    let projector = geom_api::ProjectPointOnCurve::new_pnt_handlegeomcurve(&pt, curve);
    if projector.nb_points() > 0 {
        Ok(projector.lower_distance_parameter())
    } else {
        Err(format!(
            "Failed to project point [{:.6}, {:.6}, {:.6}] onto curve",
            point[0], point[1], point[2]
        ))
    }
}

Sphere-cylinder tangent: circle on the sphere (not yet implemented)\nfn compute_tangent_edge_curve(\n    edge: &mut ReconEdge,\n    face_descriptors: &[FaceDescriptor],\n    vertices: &[BRepVertex],\n    stage2: &Stage2Output,\n    config: &Config,\n) -> Result<(), String> {\n    // Both vertex endpoints must exist for tangent edges\n    if edge.vertex_indices[0] == usize::MAX || edge.vertex_indices[1] == usize::MAX {\n        return Err(\"tangent edge has no vertex endpoints\".to_string());\n    }\n\n    let v0 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[0]];\n    let v1 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[1]];\n\n    // Determine the curve direction from surface geometry.\n    // For plane-cylinder and cylinder-cylinder tangencies, the tangent curve\n    // is a line whose direction should match the cylinder axis.\n    let [fi0, fi1] = edge.face_indices;\n    let ss0 = &stage2.selected_surfaces[face_descriptors[fi0].selected_surface_idx];\n    let ss1 = &stage2.selected_surfaces[face_descriptors[fi1].selected_surface_idx];\n\n    // Get the line direction from the cylinder axis (if one of the surfaces is cylindrical)\n    let line_dir = match (ss0, ss1) {\n        (SelectedSurface::Cylindrical(idx), SelectedSurface::Planar(_))\n        | (SelectedSurface::Planar(_), SelectedSurface::Cylindrical(idx)) => {\n            let hyp = &stage2.cylindrical_hypotheses[*idx];\n            hyp.axis_direction\n        }\n        (SelectedSurface::Cylindrical(idx), SelectedSurface::Cylindrical(_)) => {\n            // Both cylinders — use the first one's axis\n            let hyp = &stage2.cylindrical_hypotheses[*idx];\n            hyp.axis_direction\n        }\n        _ => {\n            // Fallback: use direction from vertex to vertex\n            let dx = v1.point[0] - v0.point[0];\n            let dy = v1.point[1] - v0.point[1];\n            let dz = v1.point[2] - v0.point[2];\n            let len = (dx * dx + dy * dy + dz * dz).sqrt();\n            if len < 1e-15 {\n                return Err(\"tangent edge has zero-length vertex span\".to_string());\n            }\n            [dx / len, dy / len, dz / len]\n        }\n    };\n\n    // Compute the tangent point analytically for plane-cylinder pairs.\n    // The tangent point lies on the cylinder surface at the point closest to the plane,\n    // i.e., axis_origin + r * radial_dir, where radial_dir is the comp

```
) -> Result<Stage3Output, Stage3Error> {
    let num_faces = output.face_descriptors.len();

    // 1. Create shared TopoDS_Vertex for each BRepVertex
    let topo_vertices: Vec<OwnedPtr<topo_ds::Vertex>> = output
        .vertices
        .iter()
        .map(|v| {
            let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(v.point[0], v.point[1], v.point[2]);
            let mut mv = b_rep_builder_api::MakeVertex::new_pnt(&pt);
            mv.vertex().to_owned()
        })
        .collect();

    // 2. Create TopoDS_Edge for each ReconEdge, using shared vertices when available
    let mut topo_edges: Vec<OwnedPtr<b_rep_builder_api::MakeEdge>> = Vec::with_capacity(output.edges.len());
    for (ei, edge) in output.edges.iter().enumerate() {
        let curve = edge.curve_3d.as_ref().unwrap_or_else(|| {
            panic!("edge {ei} has no 3D curve \u{2014} stage 3.3 must run first")
        });
        let make_edge = if edge.vertex_indices[0] != usize::MAX
            && edge.vertex_indices[1] != usize::MAX
        {
            // Both endpoints have shared vertices — create edge with explicit vertex topology.
            // For periodic curves (circles), MakeEdge strips the TrimmedCurve and uses
            // AdjustPeriodic which forces p1 < p2, always taking the forward arc from V1
            // to V2. We must ensure V1 corresponds to the curve's first_parameter and V2
            // to last_parameter, otherwise MakeEdge will select the complementary arc.
            let c = curve.get();
            let p_start = c.value(c.first_parameter());
            let v0_pt = &output.vertices[edge.vertex_indices[0]].point;
            let v1_pt = &output.vertices[edge.vertex_indices[1]].point;
            let d0_start = (v0_pt[0] - p_start.x()).powi(2)
                + (v0_pt[1] - p_start.y()).powi(2)
                + (v0_pt[2] - p_start.z()).powi(2);
            let d1_start = (v1_pt[0] - p_start.x()).powi(2)
                + (v1_pt[1] - p_start.y()).powi(2)
                + (v1_pt[2] - p_start.z()).powi(2);
            let (vi0, vi1) = if d0_start <= d1_start {
                (edge.vertex_indices[0], edge.vertex_indices[1])
            } else {
                (edge.vertex_indices[1], edge.vertex_indices[0])
            };
            b_rep_builder_api::MakeEdge::new_handlegeomcurve_vertex2(
                curve,
                &topo_vertices[vi0],
                &topo_vertices[vi1],
            )
        } else {
            // Closed-loop edge (no vertex endpoints) — create without vertices
            b_rep_builder_api::MakeEdge::new_handlegeomcurve(curve)
        };
        if !make_edge.is_done() {
            return Err(Stage3Error::AdjacencyError(format!(
                "MakeEdge failed for edge {ei}: {:?}",
                make_edge.error(),
            )));
        }
        topo_edges.push(make_edge);
    }

            // AdjustPeriodic which forces p1 < p2, always taking the forward arc from V1
            // to V2. We must ensure V1 corresponds to the curve's first_parameter and V2
            // to last_parameter, otherwise MakeEdge will select the complementary arc.
            let c = curve.get();
            let p_start = c.value(c.first_parameter());
            let v0_pt = &output.vertices[edge.vertex_indices[0]].point;
            let v1_pt = &output.vertices[edge.vertex_indices[1]].point;
            let d0_start = (v0_pt[0] - p_start.x()).powi(2)
                + (v0_pt[1] - p_start.y()).powi(2)
                + (v0_pt[2] - p_start.z()).powi(2);
            let d1_start = (v1_pt[0] - p_start.x()).powi(2)
                + (v1_pt[1] - p_start.y()).powi(2)
                + (v1_pt[2] - p_start.z()).powi(2);
            let (vi0, vi1) = if d0_start <= d1_start {
                (edge.vertex_indices[0], edge.vertex_indices[1])
            } else {
                (edge.vertex_indices[1], edge.vertex_indices[0])
            };

            // which can fail when vertex tolerance exceeds default precision.

            let lp = c.last_parameter();
            let (p1, p2) = if d0_start <= d1_start {

            } else {
                (lp, fp)
            };

#[test]
fn missing_output_path_error() {
    let dir = manifest_dir();
    let stl = format!("{dir}/tests/ccad/generated/cube.stl");
    let config = config::parse_config_from(["brepper", &stl, "-q"]).unwrap();
    let mesh = stage1::stage1(&config).expect("stage1 should pass");
    let surfaces = stage2::stage2(&config, mesh).expect("stage2 should pass");
    let brep = stage3::stage3(&config, surfaces).expect("stage3 should pass");
    let result = stage4::stage4(&config, brep);
    assert!(result.is_err());
    let err = result.unwrap_err();
    assert!(
        err.to_string().contains("no output STEP file path"),
        "expected MissingOutputPath error, got: {err}"
    );
}

**Intersection computation (implemented):**
- For non-tangent edges: use `GeomAPI_IntSS` to compute intersection curves with tolerance `vertex_tolerance_mm`. IntSS may return multiple curves (e.g., a plane cutting through a torus); `select_closest_curve()` picks the one closest to the mesh boundary vertices by sampling up to 10 evenly-spaced boundary vertices, projecting each onto each candidate curve via `GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnCurve`, and summing distances.
- For tangent edges: currently skipped (no tangent edges arise in the current test models). Future implementation will construct curves directly based on surface pair types.

**Trimming to vertex endpoints (implemented):**

````
## opencascade-sys API Patterns

The Rust bindings mirror OCCT C++ closely but with automatic name transformations:

### Module and Type Naming
- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeEdge` → `b_rep_builder_api::MakeEdge`
- `BRep_Tool` → `b_rep::Tool`
- `gp_Pnt` → `gp::Pnt`
- `TopExp_Explorer` → `top_exp::Explorer`
- `GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnSurf` → `geom_api::ProjectPointOnSurf`
- `STEPControl_Reader` → `step_control::Reader`
- `RWStl` → `rw_stl` (free functions)

### Constructors
Named `::new_<compressed_param_types>()`. For example:
```rust
gp::Pnt::new_real3(x, y, z)           // gp_Pnt(double, double, double)
gp::Dir::new_real3(dx, dy, dz)
top_exp::Explorer::new_shape_shapeenum2(shape, from_type, avoid_type)
geom_api::ProjectPointOnSurf::new_pnt_handlegeomsurface_extalgo(pt, surf, algo)
```

### Ownership
- All objects are returned as `OwnedPtr<T>` which auto-derefs via `Deref`/`DerefMut`
- `OwnedPtr<T>` is essentially `Box<T>` for OCCT heap objects

### Handles (OCCT `Handle<T>`)
- `Type::to_handle(owned_ptr)` consumes `OwnedPtr<T>` → `OwnedPtr<HandleT>`
- `.get()` dereferences a handle to access the underlying object
- Upcasting: `.to_handle_surface()`, `.to_handle_curve()`, etc.
- Downcasting: `.downcast_to_plane()` returns `Option<OwnedPtr<HandleDerived>>`

### Enums
Typed Rust enums, not raw integers:
```rust
top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face
extrema::ExtAlgo::Grad
```

### Shape Downcasting
`topo_ds::face_shape` is a safe function, but it panics if the shape is not a Face. Always guard with `shape_type()`:
```rust
if shape_ref.shape_type() == top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face {
    let face = topo_ds::face_shape(shape_ref);  // &topo_ds::Shape → &topo_ds::Face
    ...
}
```

### Static Methods → Free Functions
OCCT utility classes (e.g., `BRep_Tool`) become module-level free functions or associated functions on the type.

## Common API Recipes

### Read an STL file → vertex positions
```rust
use opencascade_sys::{rw_stl, message};
let progress = message::ProgressRange::new();
let tri_handle = rw_stl::read_file_charptr_progressrange_2(path, &progress);
let tri = tri_handle.get();  // &Poly_Triangulation
for i in 1..=tri.nb_nodes() {
    let pt = tri.node(i);  // OwnedPtr<gp::Pnt>
    let (x, y, z) = (pt.x(), pt.y(), pt.z());
}
```

### Read a STEP file → TopoDS_Shape
```rust
use opencascade_sys::{step_control, message};
let mut reader = step_control::Reader::new();
reader.read_file_charptr(path);
reader.transfer_roots(&message::ProgressRange::new());
let shape = reader.one_shape();  // OwnedPtr<topo_ds::Shape>
```

### Iterate faces of a shape
```rust
use opencascade_sys::{top_exp, top_abs, topo_ds};
let mut explorer = top_exp::Explorer::new_shape_shapeenum2(
    &shape, top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face, top_abs::ShapeEnum::Shape,
);
while explorer.more() {
    let face = topo_ds::face_shape(explorer.value());
    // ... use face
    explorer.next();
}
```

### Project a point onto a surface (compute distance)
```rust
use opencascade_sys::{b_rep, geom_api, extrema};
let surface = b_rep::Tool::surface_face(face);  // OwnedPtr<HandleGeomSurface>
let projector = geom_api::ProjectPointOnSurf::new_pnt_handlegeomsurface_extalgo(
    &point, &surface, extrema::ExtAlgo::Grad,
);
if projector.is_done() && projector.nb_points() > 0 {
    let dist = projector.lower_distance();
}
```

### Poly_Triangulation index convention
````
</details>

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 100-300 of <a href="../LEARNINGS.md#L100-L301">LEARNINGS.md</a></summary>

```
### Poly_Triangulation index convention
All node/triangle indices are **1-based** (OCCT convention).


### Bounded vs unbounded distance: BRepExtrema vs GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnSurf
`GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnSurf` projects onto the *infinite* underlying `Geom_Surface`, not the bounded face. Use `BRepExtrema_DistShapeShape` (via `b_rep_extrema::DistShapeShape`) instead to compute distance to bounded `TopoDS_Face` shapes. Create a `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeVertex` from the point, then compute `DistShapeShape` between the vertex shape and the target shape. This correctly reports nonzero distance for points that lie on an infinite surface extension but outside the bounded face.

### Stage 2.1 planar hypothesis algorithm
- BFS region growing from seed faces using angular + vertex-distance tolerance.
- Angular threshold of `1e-6` (1 - cos ≈ 0.08°) prevents grouping faces with slightly different normals.
- Re-fitting via area-weighted normal averaging + vertex centroid for plane distance.
- For CAD meshes, vertices lie precisely on surfaces, so fit errors are typically 0 or near-epsilon.
- Coplanar but disconnected faces correctly get separate hypotheses (not a bug).
- The `vertex_tolerance_mm` config parameter controls the maximum vertex-to-plane distance for grouping.

### CodeCAD (ccad) test model generation
- `ccad build` in `tests/ccad/` generates both STL and STEP files from Lua scripts.
- `wedge(dx, dy, dz, ltx)` requires `ltx > 0` (cannot be 0).
- Boolean operations like `union()` work with pre-translated shapes.
- Parts are registered in `project.json`; each entry needs a unique `id`.
- Available primitives: `box()`, `cylinder(diameter, height)`, `sphere(diameter)`, `cone(d1, d2, height)`, `wedge()`, `hex_prism()`.
- Boolean operations: `union()`, `difference()`, `intersection()`.
- Transformations: `translate()`, `rotate_x/y/z()`, `scale()`, `center_xyz()` etc.
- Construction: `extrude(face, height)`, `revolve(profile, angle)`.
- Edge ops: `fillet_all(s, r)`, `chamfer_all(s, d)`.

### CAD tessellation quad-strip patterns
- Tessellated cylinders and spheres from CAD tools (CodeCAD/OCCT, Onshape) use quad-strip patterns where each quad facet splits into 2 coplanar triangles.
- Solution: stage 1.3 merges coplanar triangle pairs back into quads, so curved-surface facets become single faces with single-face planar hypotheses.
- This restores the clean "single-face = curved surface candidate" criterion for stages 2.2 and 2.3.
- Sphere poles are an exception: triangles in the polar fan pattern don't have coplanar neighbors and correctly remain as triangles.

### Stage 2.2 cylindrical hypothesis algorithm
- Two-step fitting: (1) axis direction from smallest eigenvector of area-weighted normal covariance matrix M = Σ wᵢ nᵢ nᵢᵀ, (2) axis position and radius via 2D algebraic circle fit after projecting vertices perpendicular to axis.
- **Critical: center the 2D projected coordinates before circle fitting.** Without centering, the normal equations matrix becomes catastrophically ill-conditioned when absolute vertex coordinates are large relative to the arc span (e.g., vertices at (15, 12.5) spanning 2.5° of a r=2mm cylinder). The sums-of-squares terms (sx2 ≈ 1350) dwarf the signal (radius ≈ 2), causing Cramer's rule to produce wildly wrong radii (e.g., 139mm instead of 2mm). Fix: subtract mean_x/mean_y before fitting, add back when converting 2D center to 3D. This is the standard numerical remedy for polynomial least-squares fitting.
- 3×3 symmetric eigenvalue computation uses depressed cubic (trigonometric solution with cos(θ/3)), plus null-space eigenvector via cross products of (M - λI) rows.
- Multi-seed evaluation: for each starting face, collects ALL valid seed partners (not just the first), runs a trial BFS for each using temporary data (no mesh mutation during trials), keeps the best trial (most faces). Early termination abandons trials that rediscover the same cylinder as the current best.
- BFS seeds from pairs of adjacent faces with no
... (truncated, 22350 chars)
```

### Stage 3.2 tangency detection
- Surface normals at boundary points can be computed analytically for planar/cylindrical/spherical surfaces without needing OCCT UV evaluation. Plane: constant normal. Cylinder: radial direction from axis (negate for concave). Sphere: direction from center (negate for concave).
- Tangency threshold of 2° (cos ≈ 0.9994) works well. Current test models have no tangent edges since all surface intersections (plane-plane, plane-cylinder, plane-sphere, cylinder-sphere) meet at angles far exceeding 2°.
- Tangent edges will arise from fillets and blends (e.g., cylinder tangent to plane at a fillet edge).

### Stage 3.3 edge curve computation
- `GeomAPI_IntSS::new_handlegeomsurface2_real(S1, S2, Tol)` computes surface-surface intersection curves. Returns 1-indexed curves via `.nb_lines()` and `.line(i)` which returns `&HandleGeomCurve`.
- When IntSS returns multiple curves, select the one closest to mesh boundary vertices by projecting sampled boundary points onto each curve via `GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnCurve` and picking minimum total distance.
- `GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnCurve::new_pnt_handlegeomcurve(&pt, curve)` projects a point onto a curve. `.lower_distance_parameter()` returns the parameter of the closest point.
- `Geom_TrimmedCurve::new_handlegeomcurve_real2(curve, U1, U2)` trims a curve to a parameter range. Convert to handle via `Geom_TrimmedCurve::to_handle(owned).to_handle_curve()`.
- For closed-loop edges (no vertex endpoints, e.g., full circle on a cylinder cap), use the curve's full parameter range `[first_parameter, last_parameter]`.
- `ReconEdge.curve_3d` stores `Option<OwnedPtr<geom::HandleGeomCurve>>`. `OwnedPtr` does not implement `Debug`, so `ReconEdge` needs a manual `Debug` impl.
- All current test models compute 100% of edge curves successfully via IntSS (cube 12/12, cylinder 2/2, hemisphere 1/1, ball_on_cylinder 2/2, block_with_hole 15/15, pipe 4/4, spherical_pocket 13/13, chamfered_cube 48/48).
- Tangent edges will arise from fillets and blends (e.g., cylinder tangent to plane at a fillet edge).

Now add the MakeEdge learnings. Let me read the current stage 3.4 section to see what needs updating:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 200-215 of <a href="../LEARNINGS.md#L200-L216">LEARNINGS.md</a></summary>

```
- **Planar faces**: Create `TopoDS_Edge` from trimmed curve using `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeEdge::new_handlegeomcurve(curve)`. Group edges into wire loops by `BRepVertex` vertex connectivity (union-find style). Build `MakeWire` from edges, `MakeFace` from surface + outer wire + hole wires.
- **Periodic faces (cylinder/sphere) with edges**: Two approaches depending on the edge type:
  - **Full revolution** (all boundary edges are closed loops): Use UV-bounds construction via `MakeFace::new_handlegeomsurface_real5(surface, umin, umax, vmin, vmax, tol)`. This automatically creates seam edges needed for proper B-Rep topology. Circular gap algorithm computes correct u-bounds even when edges cross the 0/2π seam.
  - **Partial revolution** (open edges with vertices): Use wire-based construction WITH pre-set pcurves via `BRep_Builder::update_edge` before calling `MakeFace`. The pcurves (Geom2d_Line) map the edge's 3D parameter range to the surface's UV space. This ensures correct arc selection on the periodic surface and shares edges with adjacent planar faces for proper sewing.
- **Full spheres** (no edges): Use `MakeFace::new_handlegeomsurface_real(surface, tolerance)` which uses natural surface bounds.
- `MakeEdge::edge()` takes `&mut self`, not `&self`. Need `&mut` references when accessing edges.
- `BRepCheck_Analyzer::new_shape_bool(shape, true)` validates faces. `.is_valid()` returns bool.
- The base `geom::Surface` type does NOT have `bounds()` or `first_u_parameter()` etc. in the Rust bindings. These are available on specific subtypes (CylindricalSurface, SphericalSurface, Plane, etc.). For UV bounds, compute them from geometry (mesh vertices, hypothesis parameters) rather than trying to query the surface.
- **Shared vertices**: Create `TopoDS_Vertex` objects from `BRepVertex` positions (`MakeVertex::new_pnt(pt)` then `.vertex().to_owned()`), then use `MakeEdge::new_handlegeomcurve_vertex2(curve, &v1, &v2)` instead of `MakeEdge::new_handlegeomcurve(curve)`. This ensures edges explicitly share vertex objects, making sewing topology-aware rather than proximity-based.
- **Pcurves on periodic faces**: For partial-revolution periodic faces, pcurves must be pre-set on IntSS edges using `BRep_Builder::update_edge_edge_handlegeom2dcurve_handlegeomsurface_location_real()` BEFORE creating the MakeFace. Without pre-set pcurves, MakeFace may compute incorrect pcurves that select the wrong arc of the periodic surface.
- **CRITICAL: MakeEdge vertex order for periodic curves**: `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeEdge` strips `TrimmedCurve` wrappers and uses the base curve. For periodic curves (circles), `ElCLib::AdjustPeriodic` forces p1 < p2, always selecting the forward (CCW) arc from V1 to V2. If V1/V2 are in the wrong order relative to the curve parameterization, MakeEdge creates an edge spanning the complementary arc (e.g., 270° instead of 90°). Fix: when creating MakeEdge, ensure V1 is closest to the curve's `first_parameter()` point and V2 to the `last_parameter()` point.
- **BRepCheck_Face diagnostics**: `b_rep_check::Face::new_face(face)` creates a face checker. Call `.minimum()` first to compute all checks, then query `.intersect_wires(false)`, `.classify_wires(false)`, `.orientation_of_wires(false)` (each returns `b_rep_check::Status`), and `.is_unorientable()` (bool).
- **Wire orientation fix for holes**: After adding inner wires (holes) to a `MakeFace`, apply `shape_fix::Face::new_face(make_face.face())` then `.fix_orientation()` to ensure inner wires are oriented opposite to the outer wire. Wrap the result back via `MakeFace::new_face(&fixer.face())`. Without this, OCCT's BRepCheck reports `Badorientationofsubshape` on every planar face with holes.
